CONFIRENTIAL
ECONONIC STABILIZATION IN JAPAN
The United States Government sonsiders it a matter of urgemey
that the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers require the
Japanese Government immediately to put into effeet a program of
domestic eeonomie stabilization, including measures lesding to
fiseal, monetary, price, and wage stability, and maximum pre-
duetion for export. The United States Government further believes
that in this conaeetion the Japanese Government should be required
to adopt measures designed to:
a. Aohive a true balance in the consolidated budget
at the earliest possible date by stringent surtailing of
expenditures and maximum expansion in total governmental
revenues, ineluding such new revenue measures as may be neces-
sary and appropriate.
b. Accelerate and strengthen the program of tax
collection and insure prompt, widespread and vigorous crimimal
proseoution of tax evaders.
c. Asaure rigorous limitation of credit extension to
projeets contributing to esonomic reeovery of Japan.
d. Establish an effeetive program to achieve wage
stability.
e. Strengthen and, if necessary, expand the coverage
of existing price control programs.
f. Improve the operation of foreign trade eontrols.
Tighten existing foreign exchenge controls, to the extent
that such measures can appropriately be delegated to Japanese
agencies.
g. Improve the effectiveness of the present allocation
and rationing system, particularly to the end of maximizing
exports.
h. Increase production of all essential indigenous
raw material and manufaetured produets.
1. Improve effieieney of the food sollection program.
It is to be expeeted that the Japanese Government would
develop these measures to meet a target date for the establish-
ment of a single exehange rate not later than three months after
the 1nitlation of the stabilization program.
Conseious

The degree of validity of General MacArthur's first two as-
sumptions appears to be a very basic determinant in assessing his
conclusion that hie programfor a self-supporting Japanese economy
will reeult by l953 in achieving a balance between imports and exports,
thereby lifting from the shouldere of the U. S. taxpayer the burden of
support of the Japanese economy. These assumptions would normally be
presented in connection with the defense of the proposed appropriation
to the Congressional committees concerned. Whether they are presented
initially or not, it is certain that their substance will be raised by
the committees concerned, and that it will be necessary to express a
definitive view concerning the subject matter of these two points, and
concerning their impact on SCAp's current plan or any other plan for
achieving a self-supporting Japan.
Your views on the problem raised in the preceding paragraph are
requested at your earliest convenience for the purpose of providing
guidance for use in the defense before Congress of the economic re-
habilitation program for Japan and also for use in providing General
MacArthur with necessary advice for furtner planning. It would be
particularly appreciated if they mignt be forwarded within a week,
since the concepts involved are of such fundamental importance that
the completion of materials for distribution to Congressional com-
mittees in early January muet necessarily await such guidance.

A letter has been received from the Acting Secretary of
the Army requesting the views of the Department with regard to
two of the seven assumptions made by General MacArthur in pre-
paring the progrem for a self-supporting Japanese economy, of
which a proposed appropriation this year of $165 million is an
ossential component. These two assumptions, whioh presumably
will be discussed in connection with Congressional hearings on
the Army appropriation request, are as follows:
(1) That there will be an integrated and expanding program
of American aid for Asia;
(2) That political and economic conditions in the Far East
will permit the necessary expansion of trade.

December 28, 1948
Dear Mr. Draper:
This is in reply to your letter of December 14, 1948, re-
questing the Department of State's views concerning the first
two assumptions underlying the SCAP program for a self-support-
894.50/12-1448
ing economy:
"(1) that there will be an integrated and expanding
program of American ald for Asia;
"(2) that political and economic conditions in the
Far Kast will permit the necessary expansion of trade."
In so far as the first assumption is concerned: no basis
presently exists for SCAp's assumption in regard to "an in-
tegrated and expanding program for American aid." In fact, to
avoid any possibility of conveying a misleading impression con-
cerning this Government's present policy in the Far East to the
Congress, I would sugoest the following language as an appropriate
re-statement of this assumption: "That a considerable increase
in the export productivity of other Asiatic countries will permit
an expanding exchange of goods between Japan and these countries."
The assumption stated by SCAP is only a specification of the means
to be employed in realizing this desired increase in Asiatic pro-
ductivity; if the objective is clearly stated, as in the proposed
redraft, it would not seem necessary, or possibly appropriate, for
SCAP to specify whether its attainment will require U.S. aid.
This is an issue which is presently being studied, but has not
yet been resolved, within the Department of State, in connection
with a general review of the economic and political measures that
CS/A
might be taken by the U.S. Government in support of its objeetives
in the Far East.
let
In
Dca  
SECRET FILE
The Honorable
William H. Draper, Jr.,
Acting Secretary of the Army,
Washington, D. C.
12-1440
DISL.
SECRET

=== Page 14 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
-2-
In so far as the second assumption is concerned: The
Department of otate shares SCAp's hopes for increased political
and economic stability in the Far Rast, but cannot, of course,
prediet whether developing conditions in this regard will,
in fact, be such as to permit the necessary future expansion
of Japanese trade.
The uncertain validity of these two assumptions does not,
in the view of this Department, necessarily render inaccurate
SCAP's relatively modest export projections for FY 1950. It
does, however, raise a question as to whether it will be posaible
for Japan to attain the more ambitious goals projected by SCaP
for FY 1951-53. For this reason, I believe that apecial care
should be taken to indicate to the Congress that this SCAP
program has been reviewed and approved by the U.S. Government
only in so far as it relates to the fiscal year 1950. The
Congress should be informed that, for the fiscal years following
1950, this program represents only SCAP's estimates of possible
Japanese economic performance under certain stated assumptions.
I do not believe that the U.S. Government can support these
estimates until a more adequate basis exists for appraising the
validity of the assumptions upon which they are based.
Under the circumstances, it would seem necessary to justify
the FY 1950 Japanese rehabilitation appropriation to the Congress
as an expenditure designed to bring Japan nearer to self-support
rather than on the basis of any firm commitment that it is one of
three annual expenditures that will surely lead to a state of
Japanese self-support by FY 1953.
I will, of course, inform you promptly if decisions are
reached within the Department of State that would provide a
basis for a further appraisal of the validity of the assumptions
underlying SCAp's program concerning which you wrote me.
Sincerely yours,
Robert A. Lovett
Acting Secretary

TOP SECRET
MEMORANDUM FOR FILES
Economic Implementation of U.S. Policy with Respect to the Far East
Summary
The subject paper submitted for consideration proposes essentially
a Marshall :lan for Asia. This has been consistently opposed by the
U.S. in ECAFE on the grounds that the countries of Asia are at such a
low level of industrial development and economic interdependence that
such a proram cannot succeed, that the co ntries in question have put
forward unrealistic plans for development as the basis for such a pro-
gram, and that the cost to us is beyond our reso rces, given our other
commitments.
The subject paper, however, does not include the ECAFE countries
as such in its proposal, but omits China on the round that it is or
is likely to be Soviet-controlled ("unfriendly") and that therefore it
behooves us to concentrate our aid in the r st of the region (plus
Jaoan, South Korea and Formosa) in order to build up an economic base
ofresistance to the Soviet.
The fallacies in the paper's assumotions are (1) that China or
any part of it now is, or (on the evidence) in future is likely to be,
894.
controlled by the Soviet in any real sense of the word, and (2) that the
rest of the countries for wich aid is proposed form either a politically
or economically interdenendent and manaveable unit. Thus both of the
50
paper's fundamental assumot ons are inconsistent with the facts.
The paper also fails comolete'y to face the problem of the cost of
s ch a program to us.
12.2748
On the question of commercial policy as a possible instrument for
building un the coun ri s involved, the decis'on ultim tely rests with
their own governments, since we can only attemot to get their agreement
to conditions favor hle to tr de and investment. We can by export con-
trels prevent goods goin, to undesirable destinations but we cannot by
this means muarantee shinment of coods, or pr'orit'es for shinments, to
any specific country, since contracts and deliveries are ner,otiated be-
tween purchas r and suoplier w thout U.3. Government intervention in
any way. There rema'ns only the possibility of us'ng Exim Bank loans
and credits or other public lending agencies as a means of assuring
develooment in selected areas. We are already doing what we can to pro-
vide the region with hard currency by our purchases of rice, rubber,
tin and other commodities.
It is suggested, however, that an anpropriately conceived and applied
commercial policy towards China, where factual evidence indicates that
the issue of Soviet control is still in doubt, might secure all the ob-
jectives aimed at by the paper's proposals, at no financial cost and at
little risk, leaving the proposed policy as an alternative (besides which
there are und btedly a number ofothers) to fall back on in case of failure.
TOP SZORET

Detailed argument on these points follows:
1. The subject memorandum upon which this memorandum is a comment
states the principal basic interests of the U.3. in the Far East as
follows:
"(a) to neutralize as far as poss ble any threat to U.S.
security interests from continental East Asia;
(b) to acquire necess ry commodities for the strat gic and
economic require ents pr'marily of the United States and
secondarily of Western urove and other friendly areas;
(c) to ene ura e the develo ment on the part of the gov-
ernments c ncerned of interests and objectives which are
consistent w'th t ose of the Un'ted States."
2. The subject paper indicates as co ntries in which action is re-
quired to protect these ir terests (and by hypothesis, countries which
are "friendly"), the follow ng: (1) the Western Pacific Islands (includ-
ing Formosa), (2) South Korea, (3) Malaya, (4) Siam, (5) Indo-China,
(6) Burma, (7) India, (8) Faki ;tan, and (9) Ceylon. (Page 1). The
paper's co ntry-by-country analysis also includes Janan, the Philippines,
and the Netherlands Indies (pages 3-5), which are omitted from the first
list without explanation; an it is evident that as a matter of fact
Jaoan and the Philiopines are considered as deserving the highest pri-
ority, alon: with Sian, India and Pakistan, as potential recipients of
U.s. aid, chiefly on ro nds f their greater politica stability.
3. It appears there ore that the geogranhical scope of the problem
has not received the careful def'nition which the proposal wonld seem
to reuire.
4. By imolicution, such narts of the Far East as are not included
in the list of countries vi oh respect to ich act'on is held to be re-
quired are treated as "unfriendly" or potentially "unfriendly" countries
to be de ded any aid or assistance.
5. The "positive" econonic policy recommended for the countries
deemed worthy of our attention is to consist "in furnishing through
public or private means such canital equ' ment and other supplies as
may annear to he necessary to increase the pro ction for export of
food and raw materials needed in world markets; assisting within the
limits of our resources in uch add tional economic develooment pro-
jects as may anpear sound and self-liquidating; and encouraging greater
exchange of goods within the area." (Page 1).
It is evident throughout the paper that the essence of this
policy is conceived as a coordinated pro ram of economic assistance to
the area, along the lines of ERP; see Recommendation (b), pages 11-12.
6. Admitting for the purpose of discussion the validity of the
paper's assumptions (Paragraphs 1-4 above), concerning which some
comment

elsewhre?

vur reasons are not so frankly stated in our instruct'ons to U.S. ECAFE
Delerabions, but 6 is is tie essence of them. Cf. UNE D-100/48, Nov. 17,

7. fhe second set of facts consists of the estirates of the finan-
cial reo irerents for such a pro ram, orepared by the countries them-
selves. sCaFE Document E/CN.1l/131 (8 Novenber 194e) estimates total

of this total, the amo nt required by C ina was estinated at 6.6

GENERAL HEADQUARTERS
FAR EAST COMMAND
Public Information Office
IMMEDIATE RELEASE:
19 December 1948
SCAP LETTER TO PRIME MINISTER ON STABILIZATION
General of the Army Douglas Macarthur today forwerded the fol-
lowing letter to Prime Minister Yoshida:
"I am just in receipt of an interim directive from the govern-
ment of the United States forwarded to me in accordance with the
terms of reference of the Far Eastern Commission. This directive
establishes a series of objectives designed to achieve fiscal,
monetary, price and wage stability in Japan as rapidly as possible,
as well as to maximize production for export. Such objectives,
which are listed as an inclosure to this letter are clear and ex-
plicit, and as pointed out in the public release of the United
States State and Army Departments follow an objective pattern, the
general aspects of which have heretofore been commmnicated to the
Japanese Goverament as a means towards the ultimate desired stabil-
ity.
"The directive proceeds from the premises that the prompt
economic stabilization of Japan is a primary objective common both
to the Allied Powers and the Japanese people; that the Americam
people so long as called upon to underwrite existing deficits in
the indigenous resources required to sustain Japanese life are en-
titled to the maximized industry of the Japanese people and the
minimized loss incident to a maldistribution of available resources
or failure vigorously to produce mative raw products or curb ex-
travagance and waste in the operation of government and industry;
and that by positive Allied intervention may obstructions incident
to improvidential political conflicts, unobjective labor strife and
destructive ideological pressures best be avoided.
"The fundamental objective of this action, reduced to language
which all may understand, is the prompt achievement of that degree
of economic self-sufficiency which alone can justify and insure
political freedom. For there can be no political freedom so long
as a people's livelihood is dependent upon the largess of others.
Nor may a people fully mobilize the collective will as an impreg-
nable barrier against evil and destructive ideological pressures
and as en irrestible force toward progressively improved living
standards, if lacking in that resolute dignity which alone springs
from mastery over its own deficiencies.
"Necessarily, the action of the United States is tied in to
the problem of relief and recovery appropriations, which may be
UNCLASSIFIED
expected

=== Page 27 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to
Tokyo's Despatch No. 804
December 27, 1948.
-2-
expected in future only in ratio to progress made through the com-
bined efforts of the Japanese people toward achievement of the
stated objectives. This will call for a reorientation of Japanese
thought and action, with both subordinated to a primary purpose
common to all of the people. It will call for increased austerity
in every phase of Japanese life and for the temporary surrender of
some of the privileges and immunities inherent in a free society.
"There will be no place for interference by management or
labor with the acceleration of production, for the burden will be
shared by every segment of Japanese society. There will be no
place for political conflict over the objectives to be sought as
these objectives are stated with crystal clarity. Nor will there
be any place for ideological opposition as the purpose to be served
is common to all of the people, and any attempt to delay or frus-
trate its accomplishment must be curbed as menacing the general
welfare.
"In keeping with my long established policy, insofar as is
possible, I shall look to the Japanese Government and people for
the vigorous and faithful fulfillment of this stabilization pro-
gram. I have faith in their ability, however stern the requirement
and great the personal sacrifice, to achieve so worthy a national
goal. The course ahead may well prove difficult but its impact
upon individual life will be minimized if the burden is equalised
among all the people.
"It is my earnest hope and indeed my confident expectation
that all Japanese men and women will rally with vigor and deter-
mination to the challenge of this objective. If they do, Japan
will evolve a pattern of progressive stability for all of strife-
torn Asia to emulate. If they do mot, Japan may perish."
Very sincerely,
DOUGLAS MaCARTHUR.
OBJECTIVES SET FORTH IN INTERIM DIRECTIVE OF THE UNITED ST TES
1. To achieve a true balance in the consolidated
budget at the earliest possible date by stringent
curtailing of expenditures and maximum expansion
in total governmental revenues, including such new
revenue as may be necessary and appropriate.
2.
To accelerate and strengthen the program of tax
collection and insure prompt, widespread and vigorous
criminal prosecution of tax evaders.
3.
To assure that credit extension is vigorously
limited to those projects contributing to economic
recovery of Japan.
4. To establish an effective program to achieve
wage

wage stability.
To strengthen and, if necessary, expand the cover-
age of existing price control programs.
6.
To improve the operation of foreign trade controls
and tighten existing foreign exchange controls, to
the extent that such measures can appropriately be
delegated to Japanese agencies.
To improve the effectiveness of the present alloca-
tion and rationing system, particularly to the end
of maximizing exports.
8. To increase production of all essential indigenous
raw material and manufactured products.
9. To improve efficiency of the food collection program.
10.
To develop the above plans to pave the way for the
early establishment of a single general exchange
rate.

Economic Stabilization in Japan (SC-329/1)
The New Zealand member stated that he was not at this
time criticizing the action taken by the US Government in
issuing the interim directive but rather asking to be con-
vinced, before seeking instructions from his Government,
that the action taken had been a proper and legitimate
exercise of authority by the power which all agreed must
exert authority. He added that he had nothing to criticize
in the substance of the directive, but he could not under-
stand from whence arose the urgency which alone would have
justified the US in taking its action. The US member indica-
ted two reasons for the action of the Us (1) the necessity of
stemming the ra idly rising inflation in Japan; (2) the
immediate necessity of asking the US Congress for appropria-
tions to meet occupation expenses and of convincing Congress
that such funds were being used economically and efficiently.
C
The UK, Australian and Chinese members expressed the view

directive was a program of broad objectives rather than a
DISTRIBUTION

MEMORANDUM TO THE UNITED STATES MEMEER OF THE
FAR EASTERN COIMISSION
Subject: Economic Stabilisation in Japan (329 Series)
You are anthorized, in your discretion, to use the folloming state-
ment in replying to questions on the above subject which were reised at
the meeting of the Far Eastern Commission on 6 Janmary 1949:
At the January 6 meeting of the Far Eastern Commlssion tim
Chinese and Philippine members raised certain questions regarding a
statement made by me at the Commission meeting of December 30. In that
statement I observed that "Ras material shortages and difficulties in
finding markets for manufactured output will make it most diffioult for
Japan to exceed l930-34 levels even with maximm effort on the part of
the Japanese. Yet, unless those production levels are reached and, even,
in some cases surpassed, due to population, technological and trade pattern
changes, Japan will not be self-supporting and its deficits will fall upon
the United States taxpayer."
894.50/1-1249
My Chinese and Philippine colleagues have expresssd consern over
d
the phrase "in some cases surpassed". I should like to reassure them that
there was no implication whatever in this phrase that SCa or the U.S.
Government contemplated any compromise of the principles set forth In the
Far Eastern Commission decision dealing with "detarmination of the pesce-
ful needs of Japan." They will note that that policy deals with the
problem of Japan's consumption needs or, in other words, the standard of
living of the Japanese people. That policy was explicit in dissociating
the standard of living concept fron any arbitrary prescription for the
pattern of Japanese industry. In other words, paragraphs 2 and 3 of thst
policy make clear that the nature and sise of a Japanese industrial struo-

CS/B

should not be interpreted to imply any specific level for any particular
industry.

=== Page 33 === (confidence 0.99)
ENCLOSURE
PROPOSED STATEMENT FOR THE U. S. REPRESENTATIVE
OF THE FAR EASTERN COMMISSION
REGARDING JAPANESE ECONOMIC STABILIZATION PROGRAM
At the January: 6 meeting of the Far Eastern Commission the Chinese and
Philippine members raised certain questions regarding a statement made by me
at the Commission meeting of December 30. In that statement I observed that
"Raw material shortages and difrioulties in finding markets for manufactured
output will make it most diffioult for Japan to exceed 1930-34 levels even
with maximum effort on the part of the Japanese. Yet, unless those produetion
levels are reached and, even, in some cases surpassed, due to population,
teohnological and trade pattern changes, Japan will not be self-supporting
and its deficits will fall upon the United States taxpayer."
My Chinese and Philippine colleagues have expressed concern over the
phrase "in some cases surpassed." I should like to reassure them that there
was no implication whatever in this phrase that SCAP or the U.8. Government
contemplated any compromise of the prinoiples set forth in the Far Eastern
Commission decision dealing with "determination of the peaceful needs of
Japan." They will note that that polioy deals with the problem of Japan's
consumption needs or, in other words, the standard of living of the Japanese
people. That policy was explioit in dissociating the standard of living
concept from any arbitrary prescription for the pattern of Japanese industry.
In other words, paragraphs 2 and 3 of that polioy make clear that the nature
and size of a Japanese industrial structure capable of satisfying Japan's
peaceful needs will be affected by population growth and various other factors
and that the 1930-34 standard should not be interpreted to imply any speeifie
level for any particular industry. It is, I believe, well known to the
Commission that the eoonomic statistios being prepared by the Japanese
Government have been based upon the 1930-34 period = 100. Indipes for
industrial productivity are related to that base period. In saying that some
production levels may have to surpass 1930-34 levels if Japan is to become
self-supporting, I was merely making clear the fact that we cannot know
at this time what, exzotly, will be the pattern of Japan's economie aetivity
which will take shape as a result of Japan's efforts to develop a peaseful
self-supporting society capable of playing a part in the economie rohabilita-
tion and recovery of the Far East and the world.

=== Page 40 === (confidence 0.96)

TO
: The Secretary
DATE: January 25,
1949.
FROM : FE, Mr. Butterworth
THROUGH: S/S
suject: Background for Luncheon with Secretary Royall
SECRET
It is understood that at your luncheon tomorrow with
Secretary Royall he will wish to discuss several of the more
1mportant problems concerning relations with Japan. One of
the chief reasons for Secretary Royall's visit to Japan at
this time is believed to be hie desire to make certain that
the initiation of the economic stabilization program gets
off to a good start. As you know, he is taking with him Mr.
Norman Dodge of Detroit who will remain in Japan as financial
adviser to SCAP. General MacArthur has from the beginning
resisted the efforts of the Washington agencies concerned to
6-
impose on Japan a stabilization program and it was only after
the NAC had taken a firm stand on this matter and after an
h
interim directive had been issued that SCAP took steps to get
the Japanese Government to carry out the desired program.
55
This program is based on the re ort of the Young Mission which
went to Japan some time ago with a view to studying the prob-
lem of the establishm-nt of a commercial rate of exchange and
one of the purposes of the stabilization program is to make
possible the establishment of such a commercial exchange rate.
FE has learned informally that General MacArthur has recently
set up in Tokyo a high economic council to advise him on all
2
economic matters, composed of General Whitney as chairman and
Generals Marquette and Fox. In view of the reluctence of SCAP
to introduce the stabilizetion program, there is some reason
f
to fear thet this economic council may have been set up as a
buffer between Mr. Dodge and SCAP with a view to watering down
any recommendations which Mr. Dodge may make before they reach
General MacArthur. In view of the importance of Mr. Dodge's
mission the President has given him, with the approval of this
Department, the personal rank of Minister, and it 1s believed
that he should have direct access to General MacArthur in
order to achieve the best results. A ccpy of the stabilization
directive is attached as TAB A.
Another matter which may come up during your luncheon with
Secretary Royall is the imolementetion of NSC 13/2, which sets
forth US policy on Japan. As you know, this paper is based
upon
SECRET

=== Page 41 === (confidence 0.98)
SECRET
-2-
upon the report made by George Kennan as a result of his
trip to Japan last s ring. One of the primary purposes of
this policy is to encourage SCAP to increase the use of his
executive euthority in carrying out policy, but considerable
resistance has been met in tryin to implement this policy.
Thia resistance comes not only fron General MacArthur him-
self but from various sections of his headquarters who ap-
perently are not in accord with the policy provisions of
NSC 13/2. There has also been some difficulty in Washington
in securing agreement as to methods of implementing this
paper but it is believed that at present relations between
the Department of the Army end the Department of State in
this respect are satisfactory.
The question of United States relations toward the Far
Eastern Commission may also arise, and it is believed you
will wish to resd the statement on this problem which has
een prep:red for your policy bork. Briefly, the Department
f the Army has tak n the position that for all practical
purposes the objectives of the FEC have been substantially
fulfilled and that from now on the scope of action of that
organization should be held to a minimum. While agreeing in
general with this point of view this Department has felt
that the United States Government could not lightly ignore
or by-pess the FEC because of its internationel commitments,
but that every effort should be made through positive action
and leadership to ensure that activities of the FEC do not
infringe on the executive authority of SCAP. It is agreed
that the FEC should not interest itself in deteiled implementa-
tion but only in the determination of brosd general policies
on matters which clearly come within its terms of reference.
One of the reasons for wishin to encourage General MacArthur
to make more use of his executive authority, as mentioned in
the preceding paragra h, is that in this way it will not be
necessary for interim directives to be issued which would
then have to be filed with the FEC and would thereby give
that body a locus standi in many matters.
It has not yet been possible to obtain agreement within
the Department on the reparations program. Pending final
determination by the Department of its position on Japanese
reparations, which it is hoped may be achieved in the near
future, it is not believed edvisable to go into that question
with Secretary Royall at this time. However, you mey wish to
look at the statement in your policy book which has been
prepared on this question.
工
SECRET

INTERIM DIRECTIVE TO THE SUPREME COMMANDER
FOR THE ALLIED POWERS
ECONOMIC STABILIZATION IN JAPAN
The following interim directive of the United States
Government, serial number 96, prepared by the Department
of the Army, in accordance with Paragraph III, three of
the terms of reference of the Far Eastern Commission, has
been received from the Department of the Army for trans-
mission to you for necessary action:
"1. You will direct the Japanese Government
immediately to carry out a progrem of economic stabili-
zation by adopting wiatever measures may be required
rapidly to cchieve fiscal, monetary, price and wage
stability in Japan and to maximize production for
export.
"2. In the development of the above program
you will direct the Japanese Government to adopt
measures designed to:
a. Achieve a t rue balance in the consoli-
dated tudget at the earliest posslble date by
stringent curtailirg of expenditures and maximum
expansicn in total government revenues, including
such nel' revenue me:sures as  ay be necessary
snd appropriate.
b. Accelerate and strengthen the rogram
of tax collection and insure prompt, widespread
and virorous criminal prosecution of tax evaders.
c. Assure rigorous limitation of credit
extension to projects contributing to economic
recovery of Japan.
d. Establish an effective program to achieve
wage stability.
e. Strengthen and, if necessary, expand the
coverage  existing price control programs.
f. Improve
JCRET

f. Improve the operation of foreign trade
comrole and tighten existing forel gn exohange
controle, to the extent that such measures can
appropriately be delegated to Japanese Agencies.
E. Improve the effectiveness of the present
allocation and rationing system, particularly
to the end of maximizing expurts.
h. Increase production of all essentiel
indigenous raw material and manufactured products.
1. Improve efficieney of the food colleotion
program.
"3. The above plans will be developed to meet
: target date for the estaulishment of a single general
exchange rate not later than three montns after the
Initiation of the stabilization program by the Japanese
Ccvernment."

Since the occupat ien of the Ryukyus by United States armed
forces in 1945, the interchange of goods anong the several major
1sland groups has been strictly controlled by the Yilitary Govern-
ment and all transactiens were made only by the civi governments
and supervised by the Military Government. A compli iry factor
in this situation, featured by almost complete stane on of inter
area trade, was the disparity in the price levels between Okinewa,
with the most depressed economy, and the more nearly self-sufficient
northern and southern areas. Okinawa, having the least to trade,
required the most in goods but was unable to pay the re atively high
yen prices asked by the other islands. The lifting of priea con-
trols and lessening of inter-area tra ie barriers th roughout the
Ryukyus, as provided for by Special Proclamation No. 33, is an ini
tial step in leveling the conomies of all areas.
According to the recently-issued but undated Ryukyuan Ecoucmic
Bulletin, prepared by the Sconomics Department of the Ryukyuan Mili-
tary Government, Ryukyuan foreign and inter-area trade prospecta have
improved substantially in recent months. Reswaption of trade, it
is pointed out, will be possible fer the following two reasons:
(1) Some items, especially much-needed cnsuwmers' goods, are avail-
able in Japan for export to the Ryukyus, and are now being purchased
here under Ryukyuan Military Govornsent auspices by meens of a
special dollar fund. This furd accumulates in the Ryukyus from pay-
ment of wages to servants of occupation personnel and for money
expended in the Ryukyus by occupation personnel for Ryukyuan handi-
craft goods, and now amounts to several hundred thousand dollars.
(2) Appropriated

According to statement s made to an officer of this lission
who visited Ukinawa during the period February 14-18, 1949, there
is considerable optimism manifested on the part both f Ryukyuans
and of Military Government personnel over the immediate and long-
range effects of the introduction of the free enterprise system in
the Ryukyus. Where some six or seven nonths ago  ere were no
privately-owned stores in operation on Ckinawa, there  as been,
since issuance of Special Proclamation No. 33, some nsion in
that field. Small stores, primarily selling locally- de goods,
have been established, together with shops doing such work as watch
end clock repair, the making of household utensils out of alumimum
aircraft scrap and beer cans, at cetera.
It was also pointed out by persons intervi ewei by the efficer
of this Mission that the lifting of price controls and rationing
of indigenous commodities, as well as the lessening of inter-area
trade barriers, also as provided by Special Froclumation No. 33,
will tend to reduce smuggling between the northern lgukyuan islands
and Japan and between the southern islands and Taiwan. Contrari-
wise, thore will now be a natural tendency for goods to move from
surplus areas in the Ryukyue to Ckinawa, which suffered heavily
from the fighting which precaded the occupation of the island by
United States foroes and which later suffered from two of the most
disastrous typhoons recorded in recent history of Okinawa.

UNITED STATES MILITARY GOVERNMENT
SPECIAL PROCLAMATION NO. 33
TO THE PEOPLE OF
THE ISLANDS OF NANSEI SHOTO AND ADJACENT WATERS SOUTH
OF THIRTY (3O) DEGREES NORTH LATITUDE
WHEREAS, it is deemed desirable to provide means whereby
Ryukyuans may under certain conditions, engage in free trade on
one economic level, among themselves and with persons of other
nationalities, within their borders.
NOW, THEREFORE, I, W. W. EAGLES, Major General, United States
Army, Chief Military Government Officer of the Islands of Nansei
Shoto and Adjacent Waters South of Thirty (30) Degrees North Lati-
tude, do hereby proclaim as follows:
Article I
DEFINITIONS
In this Proclamation and for all purposes relating thereto:
a. Ryukyu Islands (the Ryukyus) are those islands of Nansei
Shoto and adjacent waters South of Thirty (30) Degr: 's North Lati-
tude.
b. A RYUKYUAN is an individual lawfully inhabiting any part
of the Ryukyu Islands who is a native thereof, or who has taken up
residence therein with the intent of remaining indefinitely.
c. FREE ENTERPRISE is the manufacture of commodities, the
buying and selling of commodities and services without price con-
trol according to prescribed regulations.
d. INTER-AREA TRADE is the licensed exchange of commodities
botween any two of the territorial areas of the four Regional Gov-
ernments; namely, Northern Ryukyus, Okinawa, Miyako and Yaeyama.
Trade between islands of the same Regional Governments is not Inter-
Area Trade for the purposes of this proclamation.
e. MARKET is any place licensed to exchange goods or servi-
oes.
f. INDIGENOUS COMMODITY is any item produced or procured from
the natural resources of the Ryukyu Islands or manufactured therein.
g.
ESSENTIAL COMMODITY is any item declared by Military
Government
UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 59 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 1
to Tokyo's Despatch No. 123,
February 25, 1949.
-2-
Government to be essential for the rehabilitation of the basic
economy of any area within the Ryukyu Islands.
h. RATION is the amount of an essential conmodity allotted
to an individual for a given period of time.
i. SERVICES are jobs requiring specialized skills such as
watch repairing, tailoring etc. offered to the public by a Ryukyuan
in a licensed market.
Article II
FREE ENTERPRISE
Any Ryukyuan will be permitted to initiate and to engage in
Free Enterprise, including Inter-Area Trade, and, will be permitted
to sell his products or services to the Ryukyuan population and
to persons of all other nationalities within the Ryukyu Islands,
except to tho se known by seller to have entered the Islands ille-
gally, subject to the following conditions:
a. Each individual or group of persons desiring to establish
a business, or to engage in Inter-Area Trade, will first apply to
the Civil Administration or Provisional Government of his locality
and receive a license in accordance with such regulations as may be
approved byMilitary Government.
b. Indigenous food and other essential commodities will be
sold without price control in Ryukyuan markets, to Ryukyuans ohly.
The rationing of essential indigenous commodities i: discontinued
but such commodities will not be available for Inter rea Trade so
long as their essentiality to the area of productio ontinues.
c. Commodities not determined to be essential by a Regional
Government and approved as such by Military Government will be sold
by Ryukyuans wdthout price or quantity control to both Ryukyuans
and non-Ryukyuans in markets located in areas which are not off-
limits and which have been previously approved by Military Government.
d. A business licensed and operated by Ryukyuans primarily
to furnish services such as tailoring, watch repairing and cob-
bling, may furnish such services to all persons without price con-
trol.
Users of such Ryukyuan services will deal with licensed
operators only. The license- may perform the work personally or
he may delegate the work to an employee.
e. A Ryukyuan desiring to sell non-essential commodities
or services to non-Ryukyuans, as well as to Ryukyuans, will es-
tablish his market at a place on a main highway which has been pre-
viously approved by Military Government and so marked. Non-Ryukyuans
will not enter any off-limits areas for the purpose of trading in
the markets located therein.
f. Ryukyuans
UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 60 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 1
to Tokyo's Despatch No. 123,
February 25, 1949.
-3-
f. Ryukyuans may engage with other Ryukyuans in Free Inter-
Area Trade in other than essential commodities, such trade to be
licensed both in area of origin and in area to which imported.
Commodities declared to be essential will not be exchanged among
the areas.
g Ryukyuans will not engage in the production, manufacture,
distribution or sale of a commodity which has been made a govern-
ment monopoly, except as agent s of such government.
Articl- III
MILITARY GOVERNMENT CIVILIAN SUPPLY IMPORTS
Goods imported by Military Goverment will be sold to Ryukyuans
on a supplementary rationed basis, determined by the difference in
the quota for the individual, as specified by Military Government,
and that portion of such quota as he may first obtain from indi-
genous goods. The distribution of such supplementary ration will
be dependent upon the availability of such imports at any given time.
Food will be supplied on a caloric basis as defined by Military Gov~
ernment Directives establishing the basic diet. Clothing and other
goods will be supplied on an item basis as prescribed by Military
Government. The price of Military Government imported goods will
be based on the prices of similar indigenous commodities or in the ab-
sence of any similarity to indigenous goods, in line with the cur-
rent level of the general economy; except in case of Military Gov
ernment goods sold to government employees, prices will be set con-
sistent with the fixed government wages, as specified in Article VII,
hereinafter.
Article IV
MEDIUM OF EXCHANGE
The only ourrency permitted for use in transactions between,
or with, Ryukyuans will be the currently authorized circulating
medium of exchange of the Ryukyu Islands. Non-Ryukyuan purchasers
will obtain such currency through channels prescribed by Military
authority.
Article V
MILITARY GOVERNMENT IMPORT-EXPORT PROGRAM
The Import-Export Board of the Ryukyu Islands will, subject to
supervision of Military Government, determine the distribution,
and be accountable for the disposition of goods except those pur-
chased with appropriated funds imported by Military Government for
use by Ryukyuans. Prices will be based on the cost of similar indi-
genous commodities or on a fair value determined by the prevailing
economic condition in the Ryukyu Islands.
Article VI
UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 61 === (confidence 0.98)
Enclosure No. 1
to Tokyo's Despatch No. 123,
February 25, 1949.
-4~
Article VI
ITEMB FOR USE BY U. S. FORCES
It shall be unlawful for Ryukyuane knowingly to buy, sell
or barter any U. S. Government-issued foods, clothing equipment
or post-exchange items intended for exclusive use by members of
U. S. Military Installations or agencies thereof, except where
specifically authorized by Military Government.
Article VII
COMMITTEE ON RYUKYUAN ECONOMY
Representatives from each of the fow Regional Governments,
appointed from officials therecf, by the respeetive Chiji thereof,
will be organized immediately as a permanent Conmittee on Ryukyuan
Economy. Such Committee will conduet Military Governnent directed
surveys periodically for the purpose of supplying statistical data
on a continuing basis which will reflect the trends in business
enterprises, production, wages, commodity prices, and living stand-
ards. The representatives of each Regional Government will act as
an independent Committee on Economy within its governmental area
and as a constituent part of one Ruykyuan-wide Committee.
The work of the Committee on Ryukyuan economy will be per-
formed by the members as a part of their regular official duties.
Travel expenses incurred by members will be reimbursable from Ryu
kyuan yen budget.
Article VIII
PENALTIES
a. Any person who, in the Ryukyus, engages in Free Enter
prise or Inter-Area Trade as defined in Article I of Special Proc-
lamation Number 33, without a license as required by said proclamation
shall, upon conviction, be sentenced to a fine of not more than
¥1, oo0, or imprisoned for not more than three months, or both.
b. Any person who, in the Ryukyus, engages in the export in
Inter-Area Trade of essential commodities as defined by applicable
Military Government Directives, shall, upon conviction, be sentenced
to a fine of not more than ¥5, o00, or imprisoned for not more than
one year, or both.
c. Any person who, in the Ryukyus, engages in the sale of non
essential commodities to non-Ryukyuans, excapt in a market licensed
for such purpose, shall, upon conviction, be sentenced to a fine
of not more than ¥5,000, or imprisoned for not more than one year,
or both.
d. Any persen who, in any area of the Ryukyus, engages in
the commercial production, distribution, or sale of a commodity
which has been made a government monopoly by applicable Military
Government
UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 62 === (confidence 0.98)
Enclosure No. 1
to Tokyo's Despatch No. 123,
February 25, 1949.
-5 -
Government Directives, except as an ut horized agent of the civil-
ian governments, shali, upon convietion, be sentenced to a fine of
not nore than ¥5, 0oo, or imprisoned for not more than one year,
or both.
e. Any per son who, in the Ryukyus, accepts into his posses-
sion any Military Payment Certificates, shall, upon conviction,
be sentenced to a fine of not mor than ¥5, 000, or impri soned for
not more than six months, or both.
f. Any person who, in the ayukyus, buys, sells, barters, or
contracts to buy, sell or barter any food, clothing, equipment,
or post-exchange items whdch were amported into the Ryukyus for
the exclusive use of the Armed Ferees, member thereof, or their
dependents, exeept through markets specifically authorized by Mil-
itary Government, shall, upon convietion, be sentenced to a fine of
not more than Y5, Ooo, or imprisoned for net more than six months,
or both.
g. In addition to the above criminal penalties, violators
of the administrative provisions of this Proclamation, may. as
an executive action of the appropriate Regional Government be sub-
ject to suspension or revocation of any license required by special
Proclamation No. 33 of October 26, 1948.
h. Any porson who receives any property what soever frem any
member of the Occupation Forces, regardless of the nature of that
property and regardless of whether the property was wiven freely
or in exchange for money, goods, or services, may,  on conviction,
be fined not more than ¥25,000 or imprisoned for not re than one
year, or both, provided that acqui sition of propert thin the
following categories shall not be considered within the prohibi.
tion of this Section.
1. Property lawfully acquired by transactions is
authorized outlets or markets under Hilitary
Government control.
2.
Property lawfully acqured as relief benefits
from authorized relief agencies under Military
Government control.
3.
Property for which the transferee has a lawful
property pass in proper form, isoued and signed
by a comrissioned of ficer of the Armed Forces
of the United States.
4
Small amounte of personal property of inad nif
icant value given for the recipients own imnedi-
ate uso as a social amanity.
Article IX
RESCISSION

The Deputy Commander for Military Government may make such

Article XI

In case of any conflict or ambiguity bet ween t ny lis), text
of this Proclamation or any other proclamation, orue, or regula-
tion issued under my authority, and any translation thereof, the
English shall prevail.

SUBJECT: Declaration of Essential Cammodities
TO
Chijis of Regional Gevernneros in the Ryukyis
1. In accordance with Article II, paragraph b, of Seecial
Proclamation Number 33. dated 26 Cctober 1948, a declaration of
essential commodities will be made y each of the Regional Govern-
ments of the Ryukyus and submitted for approval to the Malitary
Government Officer of the area concerned and to the Economic De-
partment, Military Government, Headquarters, Ruykyus Comnand.
2. A regular declaration will be submitted for approval
every six months thereafter,
3. Upon approval of authorities indicated in paragraph 1,
commodities may be added to, or subtracted from, the list of es-
sentials between the pericdic declarations as indicated by the
change of essentiality of such commodities in any of the four
government areas in which they are produced or manufactured
4. In cases of commodities of wrich a certain centage
is essential to the area of production and the balance available
for inter-area trade, such percentages will be inuicated by the
Civil Government conc erned at the time regular lists of essential
commodities are submitted and any time between regular declara
tions that the supply situation warrants.
5. Declaration and approval of the essontiality of a como-
dity, or a percentage thereof, automatically prohibits the use of
such as an item of trade with non-Ryukyuans or as an item of in.
ter-island trade until it is removed from the list of essentials.

become effective immediately.
a. In accordance with Article II, paragraph a, Special
Proclamation No. 33, dated 26 October 1948, each Civil Government
will establish a Central Licensing Office for the issuance of li-
censes to persons now engaged, or desiring to engage, in business
transactions in the Ryukyus.
b. Any individual or group of persons at present engaged
in business, including inter-area trade, will apply wi a 60 lays
from the effective date of this Directive to the Civil ministration
of his locality for a license of operation.
c. Any person or group of persons desiring to engage in
any type of business enterprise, including inter-area trade, will,
prior to the initiation of such business, make application fer and
receive a license from the Civil Government of the area in which
the busine ss is to be established.
d. The Central Licensing Office will inform the prospective
licensee of the provisions of Special Proclamation No. 33, dated
26 October 1948, at the time of application.
e. The original of each application, upon approval by the
Central Licensing Office of the Civil Government, will be stamped
idth an official government seal and given to the licensee. Such li-
cense will be prominently displayed by the licensee in his place of
business at all times. One copy of all applications will te kept on
file by the Central Licensing Office of the Civil Government to which
application was mode. One copy of all applications, upon approval
by the Civil Governments, will be forwarded to the Economies Depart-
ment, Military Government, for the purpose of review and for record-
ing of economic data given thereon.

With the eatabltahment of an exchange rate, the Board

(This Mission believes that the latter is of importance in

trade.) Additionally, import subsidies are to be granted
where the increase in the price of the imported item would be
such as to effect appreciably the genaral price level. Export

due to the drop in the export price following the establishment

future time at the established rate without the benefit of
subsidy.
The Board expects to use the profits that will accrue to
the foreign trade fund as the result of the establishment of an
exchange rate to finance such subsidization as may be considered
necessary. Additionally, profits which accrue to certain
industries as a result of the establishment of the exchange rate
are to be utilized to reduce import and erport subsidies.
However, it will be noted that the Board doss not indicate how
such profits will be captured. It 1s assumed that they would
be obtained through taxation.
The Board admits that if e singie exchange rete "in its
true sense" is to be esteblished and mmintained, rationelisation
of industry mmst be accomplished rather than the institution of
a system of subsidies, which would, in the final anelysis, leave
the present system unchanged.
In this connecticn, e member of the Bconomic Stabilization
Board informed en officer of this Mission that, on the basis
of an exchange rate of 3s0 yen to the dollar, the Board 18
planning in terme of e 10 billion yon subsidization program for
the fiseal yeer beginning April . 1949. Although the Board
estimetes that at a rete of 330 yen to the dollar it would
currently take about 33 billion yen to cover present exports for
the mert fiseal year, it is sonsidered thet a 10 billion yen
subsidy program will actuelly cover about 80 per eent of Japan's
exports. Purthermore, it is entieipated that the remmining 80
per eent ean, for the most part. effeet e readjustment if an
honest effort is mede to rationalise. In this sonneetion,
according to information available te this Mission, although
industry rocoguiaes the inevitability of the establiahment of
a single general erahange rate in the neer future, it is hopeful,
for the most part, that it will be able to obtain govermmental
subsidiss to make up the differenee where necessary rether then
to undertake a genuine overhauling of plante to get rid of, for
example, about a 25 to 50 per cent surplus personnel overhead.
Industry would prefer to retein surplus personnel and abeort it
by expanding produstion, which in turn will depend in most
instances upon increased supplies of rew materials and a final
determination
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 72 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 124,
February 28, 1949.
determination of levels of industry.
Secondly, the Board tak the position that no general
price revision, including the prices of the agricultural parity
commodities will be made after the establishment of the exchange
rate. The Board considers that a general upward price revision
can be avoided by increasing production and, at the same time,
cutting production costs. Price adjustments are to be made only
in specific instances and then only for important items. Addition-
ally, price control is to be simplified by releasing those items
which it is no longer necessary to control.
Thirdly, in order to stabilize wages, standard wages are
to be established for industries producing commodities subject
to price control, industries employing more than a specific number
(to be determined) of persons, and those to be designated, and
an increase in the standard wage will have to be approved by the
competent authorities. To implement the wage stabilization program,
which is to be predicated on the premise that the rise of real
wages shall be in proportion to the rise of labor produotivity,
an Over-all Wage Stabilization Council is to be established. At
the same time, the Board proposes that measures be taken to
stabilize at the highest level possible Japan's minimum standard
of living.
Fourthly, with respect to publio finance and the budget, the
Board plans to strike a real balance between revenue and expendi-
tures. Among other things, there is to be a drastio out in
government personnel and governmentel administrative costs, as well
as an effeotive effort made to inorease tax revenues. Publio and
private oredit is to be put on a sound basis. Within the market-
ability limit of Reconstruotion Finamce Bank bonds, loans are to
be made prinoipally to provide funds for equipment in essential
industriss. No deficit loans are to be made.
Fifthly, measures will be taken to inorease food production
through soil improvement, inoreased provision of needed materials,
et cetera. The food colleotion program is to be improved.
Sixthly, every effort will be made to attain l949 industrial
produotion goals, to improve the quality of goods produced, and
to lower produotion costs. Priority in the allocation of produo-
tion materials will be given to the mest efficient industries
producing items of superior quality at low cost. Consideration
will be given to the ratienal adjustment of the tax burden borne
by industry. Of interest in this connection is the statement
that the Board is counting on foreign investment and that due
preparatory measures, such as tax reform, will be undertaken to
facilitate suoh investment. This is of considerable importance
inasmuch as the American Chamber of Commeroe membership estimates
that if ourrent Japanese taxes were to be applied to them without
reservation, most of them would fall within the 75 to 80 per oent
bracket and would be driven out of business. of further interest
is the Board's
CONTIDENTIAL

=== Page 73 === (confidence 0.97)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 124,
February 28, 1949.
is the Board's statement that, as unemployment develops as 
result of a genuine rationalization of undustry, measures w!ll
be taken to absorb the unemployed in public works and to expend
unemployment insurance.
Finally, all possible measures are to be taken to increase
the production of export goods. Immediate attention will be
given to the revision of the materials allocation system and
steps taken to prevent diversion to domestic consumption of raw
materials imported for the export industry. The Board plans a
drastic simplification of import-export procedures. Japan's
balance of payments position is to be improved by means of active
exploration of foreign markets and more favorable terms of trade.
Additionally, in response to foreign demand, efforts will be
made to increase exports of producer goods in addition to the
types of items exported at present. Again, as indicated heretofore,
every effort will be made to rationalize export industries,
particularly the weak and inefficient.
To date this Mission has no information concerning Mr. Dodge's
or General Marquat's reaction to the Boonomic Stabilization Board's
plan as outlined above. With the possible exception of the wage
stabilization proposal of standard wages, this Mission is of the
opinion that the plan will be well received. In regard to export
subsidies, although General Marquat has stated that there will be
no export subsidies as such, the Bconomic and Scientific Section
does recognize the need for interim subsidization in certein
instences following the establishment of an exohange rate; however,
it is being desoribed as a proposed program of "domestio subsidize-
tion" to industry within the ITo Charter's definition of domestio
aubsidy.

Outline of Over-all Measures
E. S. B.
10 February 1949
In conformity with the principles set forth in the Nine
Objectives concerning economic stabilization, the following
measures shall be taken to establish a stabilized and self-
supporting economy through the perseverence and efforts on the
part of the entire nation.
1. Establishment of exchange rate
(1) A single exchange rate shall be established in the
month of
this year.
(2) The rate of exchange shall be determined in the light
of the actual state of the over-all national economy and of the
possible extent of the cushioning meesures stated below. The
rate of exchange shall be such that it renders possible the
rationalization of enterprises end the stabilization of domestic
price level whioh are required for the participation in the
international economy end thet it invites no internal or external
apprehensions over its meintenance.
(3) The profits which acorue in the Trade Fund in con-
sequence of the esteblishment of e single exchange rete shall
be utilized to finance the following cushioning measures as a
temporary step pending the rise of production level and comple-
tion of enterprises rationalization, in order to alleviate the
effects of the single rate upon the domestis price level and
export trade. The amount to be utilized for such purpese shall
be limited to the necessary minimum so that the profits should
as far as possible be utilized for econcmic rehabilitetion.
a. As to the imports of foods and other articles, the
price rise of which directly affects the cost of living to a
largs extens, import subsidy shall be paid to such imports in
order to keep their price at the domestic level.
b. Import subsidy shall not in general be paid to such
articles which are imported primarily as rew materials for
export products.
0. As to the imperts of articles other than e. and b.
above, import subsidy shall be paid only in case where the rise
of import price is so large that, in spite of the rise of
operation
CONVIDENTIAL

=== Page 75 === (confidence 0.98)
Enclosure to Tokyo's
Despatch No. 124,
February 28, 1949.
-2-
operation of enterprises, it gives substantial effects upon
the price level.
d. As to export, subsidy shall be paid only to such
enterprises whose export is rendered econcmically impossible
on account of the fall of the export price but which will in
future be able to export at the established exchange rate and
within certain specified amount and period.
e. Large amount of export profits, which may accrue
to specific industries in consequence of the establishment of
single exchange rate, shall be utilized to reduce the import
and export subsidies.
f. The established single exchange rate shall by all
means be maintained. With the single exchange as an index,
rationalization of home industries shall be vigorously expedited
with the object of hastening the change-over to a single exchange
rate in its true sense of the term by gradually reducing import
and export subsidies.
2. Adjustment of Price Structure
(1) Raising of price level at this moment will, in principle,
be evpided as much as possible by means of the rationalization of
enterprises and the increese of production. In view of the fact
that the prevailing price structure appears to be unbalanced in
some respects and that it is necessary to align it to international
price, price adjustment will be made only in connectiom with
specifio important industries. but no general price revision will
be made.
(2) Price adjustment will be made in such a way as is will
not imperil the outlook of production increese end she maintenance
of produotion equipment. And as regards the industries which can
absorb the reperoussion of inoreased cost on consumer price, price
raise will be restrained or sube dies will be redueed.
(3) The price of agricultural produote shall be determined
according to the parity system. Efferts shall be mede, however,
to avoid as far as possible the price rise of parity price
commoditios.
(4) The price of such commodities the state of demand and
supply, operation rate, or other preduction conditions of which
have improved beyond these whieh are foreseen under the present
price structure shall be reduced, thereby contributing to the
stabilisation of the price level.
(5) As to the payment of the price adjustment subsidies,
the level of the stabilisation band shall be reezemined in the
1ight
CONPIDENTIAL

light of the international and domestic price levels.
(6) Further efforts shall be made for the enforcemsnt of
price control. Price control, at the same time, shall be
simplified by releasing such items which are no longer necessary
to control.
3. Wage stabilization
Corresponding to the policy of maintaining price level, the
following measures shall be taken for wage stabilization through
the cooperation of labor and management on the basis of the
principle that the rise of real wages shall correspond only to the
rise of labor productivity.
(1) Standard wages shall be established by industries which
shall be the national basis of wages.
(2) Approval of the competent authorities shall be required
in case where the enterprise is going to pay wages higher than
the standard wages or where the enterprise which is currently
paying wages higher than the standard wages is going to raise
them, provided that prior approval may be replaced by report to
the competent authorities in case where the former is not deemed
necessary.
(3) In case of report mentioned in the proceding paregraph,
the enterprise shall be required to prove that the souroe of
payment of wages is neither illegal nor improper.
(4) The souroe of payment of wagem shall be fully examined,
and investigation on the spot shall be conducted if necessery.
In case where the source is found to be inappropriate, necessary
adjustment shall be made with respect to subsidies and price, and
wage adjustment order, moreever, may be issued when deemed specially
necessary.
(5) The enterprises which are subject to the wage stabiliza-
tion measures shall be the predusers of the commedities of whish
official prices are set whe heve nermally empleyees over certain
numbers and others whe are ongaged in the industries te be
designated.
(6) In carrying ous the wage stabilization measures, the
Over-all Wage Stabilisation Counoil (tentetive denemination)
shall be established to be charged wish the researoh inte and
deliberation on necessary mattors.
(7) Measures shall simultaneously be takon te stebilise
people's living, such as the securing of the distribution of
foods, daily necessaries and commodities nesessary fer work, the
adjustmens

(3) In order to stabilize the dietary life of the people,
measures will be taken to achieve efficient utilization of food
and to improve efficiency cf rationing and consumption on the
basis of review of the present control system.
6. Production increase and industrial rationalization

TO
: PL - Mrs. Margaret R. I. Carter
DATE: February 28, 1949
SUBJECT:
Paper on Japanese Recovery Program
Attached are three documents relating to a project started in the
summer of 1948 for the purpose of providing factual material as back-
ground for the discussion of the economic recovery appropriation for
Japan in the fiscal year 19:0. These documents include:1) dummy prepared
in CS/s for printed pamphlet, 2) text of pamphlet, and 3) selected statistics
of the Japanese economy, draft, for use as appendix to pamphlet.
As I told Dick Friedman on the phone this afternoon, the turn-over
in the staff of the House f Foreign Affairs Committee has led to doubt
as to whether the Comnittee will issue the proposed pamphlet. The
question of whether or not to issue this pamphlet is currently under
discussion but there is no assurance that this material will be used by
the Committee at all.
In view of the considerable amount of material that has been assembled,
the question has b en raised as to whether PL would find this material of
894.50
sufficient value to warrant the issuance of a pamphlet like this or
whether PL would like to issue this material in some other form.
Although most of the work for this project has been done anonymously
in the Department of State, both the Army Department and SCAP have been
involved in planning and designing, und SCAP has provided some research
assistance. In view of this background and in view of the Army's interest
2
in this subject, it may be that State and Army might want to put out some-
thing along these lines if the House Committee should decide not to.
While we are waiting to heer the decision of the House Foreign Affairs
Cormittee I thought you might want to have a look at this material, in view
of the interest Dick Friedman showed in it today.

Dear Mr. Butterworth:
It was very pleasant indeed to lunch with you the other day and to
explore further our ideas concerning Japan. It does seem to me that the
Department of State and the members of our Council on Japan find them-
selves in very nearly complete agreement on most of the points involved.
894.50/3-549
We will soon, I hope, be in a position to help create public interest
and understanding for the policies which seem to us so suited to the ad-
vancement of the interest of the United States and to aiding the Japan-
ese to develop along lines both in their interest and in ours. I will,
in accordance with our conversation, pass on to you from time to time
information and reports which seem of interest to us and which might
also be of interest to the State Department.
I am attaching herewith a copy of a brief report sent to me by
Mr. Pakenham, Newsweek's correspondent who is now in China. He stopped
over in Tokyo for a few days on his way to Shanghai and gathered this
information. While there is nothing unusual about it, it struck me as
a balanced and interesting assessment. I am also attaching the account
we discussed of the events of August 14, 15, 1945. Mr. Allison and Mr.
Bishop might be interested in reading this too, and if you like, please
pass it on to them. I will ask that it be returned to me when you have
finished with it, since I have only two copies.
xr
894.50
I am also attaching a copy of the report on the Japanese coal indus-
try drawn up by Mr. Antonin Raymond, a member of our Council. While it
894.6362
would not be proper for the Council to advocate or support Mr. Raymond's
commercial proposals, I feel that it is in the interest of all concerned
DC/R
for us to help such information reach the proper places, and it is in
2
Sω
that sense in which I commend this to your attention. Mr. Raymond will

=== Page 94 === (confidence 0.98)
Enclosure No. 1 to Despatch No. 149
from the United States Political Ad-
viser for Japan, Tokyo, dated March
14, 1949, on the subject "SCAP Announces
UNCLASSIFIED
New Procurement Policy".
(COPY)
GENERAL HEADQUARTERS
FAR EAST CONLAND
Public Information Office
1330
Press Release:
21 February 1949
INVOKE STRICT ECONOHY MEASURES FOR OCCUPATION REQUIREHENTS
With an eye to the stabilization of the Japanese economy, SCAP
Note:
has issued instructions to invoke a new and stiffer procurement pol-
icy.
A recent review of the current procurement policy has indicated
that certain revision toward reduction of items of procurement and the
institution of stricter controls will keep to a minimum the financial
NSc polinn
impact of the Occupation Forces on the Japanese economy.
In the fi ture, directives to the Japanese Government wdll include
only those requirements for the Occupation Forces which would normally
be furnished Zone of Intericr forces through the expenditure of appro-
priated funds. Any other procurement, unless specifically authorised
by SCAP, will be on a reimbursable basis. No procurement demands will
hereafter be initiated for the purpose of raising above existing stand-
ards of Occupation Force's services or facilities.
Procurement demands, when necessary, will be issued for essential
requirements only when the requirements are such that they cannot be
fulfilled from assets or means already available. Supplies, materials
and equipment will not be procured from the Japanese economy when they
can be obtained by requisition from the United States. Supplies, 
quipment and materials excess to the authorised needs of the Occupation
will be released to the Japanese Government as soon as practicable.
Manning tables and manning levels for all types and categories of
Japanese labor are being critically re-examined with a view to effect-
ing substantial reductions A review of the Japanese labor situation
will be made whenever possible to effect a maximum reduction in numbers
and to limit or eliminate entirely overtime payments except for domes-
tic servants. Indigenous labor will not be employed to perform the
functions of military personnel in combat organisations, except to the
extent that the employment of civilian personnel by such organisations
is authorized by regulations.
Any new constructions will be limited to only the most essential
operational requirements of the Occupation Forces which cannot be met
by any other expedient. Naintainance will be provided only for those
facilities occupied by the Occupation Forces and standby maintainance
for facilities definitely required for future occupancy. The number
of dependent housing units in Japan is now firm and no additional con-
struction will be authorised at the expense of the Japanese Government.
-0-
UNCLASSIFIED

I have the honor to forward a translation of significant
LABOR
excerpts from a policy address delivered to the Diet on April 4,
894.50/4-549
NSRB
1949 , by Mr. Takayoshi AOkI. Direotor General of the Economic
Stablization Board, covering the economic policies of the Yoshida
Cabinet. This translation was published in the April 5, 1943 is-
M
use of the Hippon Times, Tokyo.
Mr. Aoki stressed the necessity of adhering to SCAP's Nine-
point Economic irective of December 19, 1948. To attain a bal..
anced budget, he said, "badly needed" expanditures for public worlks
have been elininated and price subsidies drastically curtailed.
Every effort will be made to collect taxes "under the existing
tax system, " he is quoted as stating, while steps will be taken
to equalize the tax burden and to atilize appropriated funds ef-
ficiently.
A yen counterpart fund will be established to cover all
Unitel States aid pn ropriationa , lir. Aoki said, the "original
purport" of those up ropriations had not been realized owing to
their having heretofore been "used without restriction".
Mr. Aoki urged voluntary savings for the purpose of capital
accumulation. With United States aid, he said, sufficient funds
shuuld be available for maintenance of capital and for economic
rehabilitation, although, as the importance of the Reconstriction
Finance Bank diminishes. measures must be taken to obtain the "vol-
untary cooperation" of private banks toward proper channeling of
capital.
Regarding the price structure, Mr. Aoki said that no general
upward revision is contemplated at present. Although subsidies
SEP
are undesirable, he said, they are necessary to prevent severe
FILED
dislocations and to cushion the intiiel shock of a single exchang
