PUBLIC RELATIONS ASPECTS OF THE ECONOMIC STABILIZATION PROCRAM IN JAPAN
During the past few months, the economic stabilization program has met
with widespread opposition from the major segments of Japanese society -- op-
position that is having adverse political repercussions and is a potential
threat to the successful implementation of the stabilization program and to
the prestige of the United States occupation.1 While a dogree of opposition
to certain aspects of the stabilization progrem having immediate adverse ef-
fects on the special economic interests of labor, business, and farmers is
unavoidable, much of the opposition can be traced to a lack of underatanding
of the over-all economic situation facing Japan as well as of the basic ob-
9
jectives of the stabilization program and to a strong tendency to overeatimate
0.5
adverse effects of the program and underestimate its long-run benefits.
It is believed that there is a need (1) to institute an educationd
program to demonstrate the necessity for a stabilization program and its basio
contributions to the economic recovery of Japan and to a higher standeni of
living in future years; (2) to make use of all possible economic incentives
1
and ameliorative measures within the fremowork of the stabilization program
in order to reduce the opposition arising from labor, unemployed groups, and
small and modium-sized business to manageablo proportions, end, if possible,
vin the full cooperation of these groups.
A. Educational Program
Since there is a wide lack of understanding of economic stabilization
in Japan, the fundamental objective of the educational progrem would be to
uN
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 503 === (confidence 0.98)
CONFIDRNTIAL
2
demonstrate that the achievement of economic stability is an important and
necessary step toward the eventual attainment of econcmic self-support and of
optimm recovery under conditions of self-support. Beyond establishing the
over-all nocessity and value of the stabilization program to the Japanese eco-
nomy, the educational material might be geared to correlating the self-interest
of the Japanese worker, businessman, and farmer in the achievement of oconomio
stability through pointing out the stabilization program's effectivenvss in
maintaining the present standard of living and in securing prospective im-
provements in living standards. Furthermore, the oducational program might
actively counteract the unfavorable critioiam of the role of the US in in1-
tiating a stabilization program with some onerous aspects by demonstrating
that (1) the adverse effects are in fact minimized by US aid; (2) the US is
protecting the various economio groupe affected by enforcing equitablo dis-
tribution of the burdens of stabilization; and (3) the uS is basically ic-
terested in marimizing the Japanese standard of living under present ni future
world trade conditions and not in limiting Japanese exports and tho lorel of
industry to the detriment of the Japanese economy.
A clearer understanding of the stabilization progrem and the echieve-
ment of wider support for its implementation would be gained through informa-
tion relating to and explanation of the following:
1. The present economic status of Japen and the Far East end tlu re-
lation of both to the world eccnamy.
2. The role of the S in bolstering the Japanese ecanomy and the
standard of living since the end of the war.

May 2. 1949
RESUME OF PRIVATE OPINION
.......The sending of Mr. Dodge to Japan is aimed at stabilising the
Japanese economy and alleviating the burden of the American Tax payers.
In order to achieve his aim, boosting of industry, partioularly inorease
of export is of imperative necessity. To gain this goal, however, it
goes withou saying that the enforcement of the Dodge budget only is
not sufficient and it is extremely important that the following measures
should be undertaken:
1)
A firm attitude towards Communiet movements.
2)
Tignting of the Labor Standard Law.
3)
Adjustment of excessive laborers already existing and those
to be expeoted by the Enterprise Reorganization and Reconstruo-
tion Plan.
4)
Un-Employment measures.
5)
Food problems and expansion of fishing districts.
6)
Ship building and chartering of ships to increase revemues
from invisible trade. (approximately 20% of foreign trade
is wasted for freight charges.)
7)
Release of Reparation problems.
8)
Authorization of Over-sea detachments of diplomats andoivilians.
9)
Lifting or the purge, particularly for those formally engaged
in industry and foreign trade.
10)
The invitation to Japan of an influenoial, industrial adviser
and a capable adviser, well informed of industrial and
economical problems, possessing consolidated politioal power
(this may be made on the burden of Japanese government).
Of the above, the itens of critical importanoe and whioh must
be realized are:
1) A stern attitude towards Communist movement and Labor movement.
2) The invitation of an adviser possessing consolidated politioal
cower and the influencial industrial power.
3) Food Problem.
It is only when the above three are undertaken in parallel to
implementation of Dodge's draft budget, which tackles the matter from
inancial view point, that the objeot of U. S. A., the self-support of
Japanese economy in the near future can be expected. However, should the
implementation of the Dodge budget only be enforced, negleoting other
measures, there is great possibility of the program ending in failure
and fear of leading Japan into a further chaotic state.
15

=== Page 513 === (confidence 0.97)
- 2 -
Although there seems tc be some people in the American government,
who underestimate the rapid development of Communist movement in Japan
and its results, the activity of the Communists is inoreasing every day.
It is no exaggeration to say that those who enjoy the freedom of speech
and action today are the Communists. The National Government in China,
the country which is most intimately related to our country, is being
pushed into the Kwangtong and the Formosan districts, and the dominance
of the Communist party in China over a greater part of China is a matter
of course. Nothing definitely can be said yet about the future of Japan.
However, should the Dodge program end in failure unfortunately, it oan be
expeoted that there will be a great confusion of thoughts and with the
Japanese manifesting their weak points, Japan will turn Red. Thus unless
the prompt, firm and thorough aid from America is extended, there is every
fear that Japan will fall into the same fate as China.
The Far Eastern countries are fearing the rehabilitation of Japan.
But Japan, which has no resources, is completely powerless, and the fate
a
of Japan is in the hands of thers, should the supply of resouroes be out.
Japan's strong point is her strategically important location and her
industrial capacity. Only by effectively and adequately utilizing the
above advantages, do I firmly believe that Japan could become the
barricade for Communist movements in the Far East, which is being expeoted
from U. S. A., and become the supplier of manufactured goods in the Far
East, that is to say, to undertake the important role of acting as a
stabilizing factor of peoples of the Far East.
Tre Lnc

Shinjuku-ku, Tokyo, Japan.
May 31, 1949.
FAR EASTERN
My dear Mr. Grew:
I received your letter dated April 18 on the 24th of the same month.
"Ten years in Japan" has, as expected, proved to be one of last vear's
best sellers. How widely your book was read by the Japanese of all classes
is shown by the fact that it ranked 4th in the number of issues. The book
that ranked first was, if my memory is correct, an article written by
Dr. Nagai regarding his experience of receiving an atom bomb in Nagasaki.
Although I deeply regret your retirement from the diplomatic service,
I sincrely feel grateful to hear directly from you that you are still
having various responsibilities and duties and are loading a busy life,
particularly to hear that one of the mcat important jobs you are undertaking
at present is to provide a constant and a practical solution for the various
problems that Japan is currently facing. Morever, the efforts you are
extendir.g by exchanging opinions with various circlas and by so doing deep-
ening the understanding towarde Japan is a matter which all the Japanese
are feeling grateful for. It is my desire to have your activities known by
all your old friends in Japan and to have them join in in toasting for the
health of the "best friend of Japan".
Contrary to the position you are enjoying, I have been purged and,
regrettable as it may be, I am leading a quite life, a life which they call
in Japan farming when fine and reading when raining, as described in my
last letter. However, I heve occasional chances to meet Mr. Sebald and I
herein enclose a resume of the talk with him on the day before hie recent
return to the States for your personal perueal. I have presented my
private opinion in the most frank manner in view of the extremely difficult
position which Japan is now placed. Thinga, I fear, are turning out be as
I had expected. As described in the "Bulwark of Asia", the last book
written by the late Mr. Vaughn, the general Manager of U.P. in Asia, who
unfortunately died through an accident on Jan. 30 this year, current Japan
is quite different from the Japan of several years ago, while the Japanese
themeelvee have changed completely------a fact which I think you are one
of those to be most surprised. The Japanese, at present, have lost all
hope for the future and have no definite idea of their own. I regretfully
have to admit that this trend is stronger amongat the youth. There is no
doubt that the overwhelming majority of the Japanese are and will be the
most faithful ally of America, and in this respect I can not agree with
Mr. Owen Latimore's article "Japan is nobody's ally" published in the
April edition of the magazine "The Atlsntic". Nevertheless, should mis-
taken measures and methods be taken towards Japan, no one can guarantee
that Japan will not be swept into unexpected directions, because after all
nearly eighty million Japanese are confined to four small ialands with in-
adequate food, while on the other hand, comnunism is infiltrating into
China, our neighbor, and other countries in the Far East. Should there be

=== Page 515 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
a general election in the near future, it is generally agreed that the
communist will gain at least a hundred seats. Although Japan is ardently
desirous of maintaining peace and neutrality in all times, I do not
hesitate in saying that her geographical situation in Asia makes such an
absolute impoesibility. She will no doubt either have to lean towarde
America or Russia.
As noted in my resume, I sincerely desire the dispatohment of an in-
fluencial and broad viewed person having experience in handling industrial
and financial problems and who posesses a coordinated and a wide range of
political knowledge to be an adviser of the Japanese Government together
with hie attondants. A great number of experts have been diapatched to
Ja. an nitherto. The opinions and recommendatione from such experts
certainly suited the iniividual probleme covered within their respective
fielde, particulariy up to the ond of ly4s when Amorica was intending to
treat Japan in accordance with the lines of the Fotzdam Declaration.
However, I feel they have become unsuitable since the change in the
American policy in ly46. Unless a coordinated adjustment is mnde, taking
into account the influence to be extended in other circles by such opinions
and recommendations, it is rather doubtful whether the results of such
recommendations were of great value from a view point of the Japanese
rehabilitation.
Considering my purged position I think I have gone a little too far.
Although the relation between Japan and America resulted in the most
regrettable and unfortunate coneequence due to the inability on my part,
I am still sincerely desiring to see the recovered and further improved
relationehip between the two countries and the conolusion of an agreement
desirable to our mutual interests. I have written this letter motivated
by such feelings as the above which I hope you will understand.
Please send my best regards to Mrs. Grew.
Sincerely yours,
1./02.rda

=== Page 518 === (confidence 0.95)
In reply refer to
FN
My dear Mr. Littlejohn:
Reference is made to your letter of June 17, 1949 inquiring
894.50/6-1749
whether the recent Dodge Mission to Japan has or intends to publish
a report on their recommendations.
The Dodge Wission does not contemplate publishing a comprebensive
report of the character you probably have in mind. As you may know,
Mr. Dodge went to Japan as financial dviser to General MacArthur
primarily for the purpose of making recommendations respecting the
implementation of the stabilization program in Japan which General
MacArthur had issued to the Japanese Government on December 17, 1948.
Inasmuch as the purpose of the Dodge Mission was primarily that of
implementation of established policy rather than to make an economie
survey of the Japanese economy, the work of the Mission did not lend
itself to presentation in a formal published report. While in Japan
Mr. Dodge issued certain press releases summarizing the measures
which he considered essential for the successful execution of the
stabilization program. I regret that I do not have copies of these
releases available here but they may be obtained from General Head-
quarters, Supreme Commander of the Allied Powers in Tokyo. If you
have any specific questions you would care to raise regarding the
stabilization program in Japan or other financial and economic matters
relating to the occupation, I vould be pleased to endeavor to obtain
the desired information for you.
Sincerely yours,
194
0gm
Orville J. McDiarmid
Associate Chief
Division of Financial Affairs
Mr. Edward Littlejohn,
Burroughs Adding Machine Company,
Detroit 32, Michigan.

=== Page 550 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclesure No. 1 to Despatch No. 457,
dated July 15, 1949 frem the United
States Political Adviser fer Japan,
CONFIDENTIAL
Tekyo, en the subject "Forwarding
Memorandum of Conversation."
(COPY)
THE FOREIGN SERVICE
OF THE
UNITED STATES OF AMERICA
MEMORANDUM OF CONVERSATION
July 8, 1949
SUBJECT:
Current Economic Problems in Japan
PARTICIPANTS: Mr. Ormond FREILE,
Chief, Industry and Cemmerce Section,
Far Eastern Affairs, CAD,
Department of Army
Mr. David M. MAYNARD,
Econemic Counselor,
Diplomatic Section
Mr. Freile is shortly departing for the United States and
suggested a conference. He stated that he was anxious to know my
personal point of view on a number of general and certain specific
questions, currently of special interest to the Economic and
Scientific Section, General Headquarters.
GENERAL
1. (a) I stated to Mr. Freile that I believed that the
general economic situation in Japan is much better than reflected
by any of our currently published indices; that the standard of
living of the majerity of Japanese was much better compared to their
standard of living before the war, than was usually admitted by our
various Divisions. Mr. Freile stated that he agreed with this en-
tirely and also reiterated the statement that the Japanese people
as a whole were much better off than we usually reflected in sta-
tistical measures.
(b) Mr. Treile stressed the fact that the great problem
of the nert year will' be to increase sales, or in shert, to sell.
During the past three years a great deal of effort has heen expended
on getting the wheels of industry rolling and te increase production.
This phase of rehabilitation has been largely successful and we now
can produce in many lines much more than we are currently selling.
I agreed
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 551 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 1
to Tokyo's Dospatch No. 457,
July 15, 1949.
- 2 -
I agreed with Mr. Freile in this statement and stressed to him the
obvious point that we must send out Japanese salesmen and reflect
seriously on adopting a more realistic pricing policy.
(c) Mr. Freile asked my confidential opinion of the present
organization of ESS and the economic work in Japan, stating that he
was recommending a sharp cut in the ESS Staff, and further stating
thet he felt a basic reorganization should take place with the present
unwieldy ESS Section broken into several parts, as for example, a
Financial Section, a Trade Section and an Industrial Section. His
idea also included a civilian economic adviser at the Chief of Staff
level who would coordinate the various sections concerned with
sconomic problems.
I told him that my thoughts were in the same direction, but
went much farther and informed him that I believed that not only
segments of ESS but also segments of the Natural Resources Section
and other Sections should be brought into a co-egual status under a
civilian economic adviser at the Chief of Staff level. In other
words, instead of NRS we should have a Division of Agriculture and
Forestry and another of Mining and that ESS should be broken up into
such divisions as Financial Division, Division of Commerce and a
Labor Division, a change which would make the local Divisions roughly
correspond with the various department in Washington. I further
stated that, as compared to the 2200 persons now concerned in eco-
nomic activities in General Headquarters, I would like to see a slash
of 95% by January 1, 1951. That we should get further away from
operating the Japanese econamy, and maintain,an advisory capacity
with certain financial controls. In other words look toward the post
peace treaty stage. Mr. Freile replied that he agreed in general,
although perhaps not in detail. That there should be a definite over-
all reorganization and he agreed with my position that such a re-
organization should look toward the goal of being absorbed into a
normal foreign Mission, that is, an Embassy, sometime in the not too
distant future.
2。 TRADE ARRANGEMENTS
We both agreed that we did not like bi-lateral trade arrange-
ments in principle, but felt that there had been no other way in 1948
and for that matter in l949, to get trade started and increase the
level of Japanese trade. Although it was easy to be critical, no
one either in Tokyo or Washington had been able to suggest any better
alternate than the trade arrangements negotiated. We were both
convinced that with the sterling area trade would have been appre.
c:ably lower during the past year without the sterling area agreement.
We further agreed that, although there were very great difficulties
to overcome in renegotiating the sterling agreement, (realizing the
current position of England vis-a-vis dollar trade and the position
of convertibility), that it would be very unfortunate if the sterling
area agreement
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 552 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 1
to Tokyo's Despatch No. 457,
July 15, 1949.
-3-
area agreement could not be renewed. We further agreed that all
trade arrangements concluded should look forward to mmltilateralism
and felt that the clauses included in recent arrangaments with
Argentina and other South American countries made such an erpansion
possible. ESS has a very important job renegotiation and implement-
ing these trade agreements.
3. FLOOR PRICES
An exchange rate having been set, we both felt that it was
no longer necessary to maintain floor prices ane both felt that the
floor pricing system should be abolished. At the same time it must
be stressed to the Japanese Government that they must not permit any
justifiable accusations of "dumping", that is, selling for export
belaw domestic prices or below cost. We also agreed that an important
job to be done was to convince the Department of Army, Department of
State, Department of Commerce and especially the Department of
Commerce Regional Officers who daily contact American businessmen
that although Japanese competition would be keen it would not consti-
tute "dumping". That under general policies we could not "have our
cake and eat it too"--that is, we must either let the Japanese export,
or we must go on subsidizing the Japanese economy out of the American
tarpayer s pocket.
4. PRIVATE IMPORTING
We both agreed that every effort should be made to speed up
agroement on details of the mechanism to permit private importing.
We also agreed that one of the most difficult problems in this fon-
nection was to get a clarification from the Department of Army as to
exactly what they intended to buy for Japan. It appears to be
impossible to develop a sound import licensing procedure unless some
definite idea as to the Army's purchasing intentions is known.
5. INCENTIVE OR RETENTION PLAN
We both agreed that the plan for the retention of a small per-
centage foreign exchange by the Japanese exporter was a good thing.
It is very essential that Japanese should be allowed to go abroad to
sell. We both agreed that it would be sound to advance funds to
reputable concerns for this purpose to be paid later by money earned
under the retention plan system.
6. MOST FAVORED NATION TREATLENT FOR JAPAN
Mr. Freile had read various papers I have prepared on this
subject and stated that he was in substance in agreement that Most
Favored Nation treatment for Japan was important. However, he agreed
that there were a great many other problems far more important and
furthermore indicated that without a crystalization of the policy of
the Japanese Government and/or SCAP in reference to Japanese import
tariff that any bargaining on a technical level was, at this time,
premature and futile.
7. SHIPPING
CONFIDENTLAL

=== Page 553 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 1
to Tokyo's Despatch No. 457,
July 15, 1949.
-4-
7.
SHIPPING
We both agreed that Japan should have more shipping at its
disposal so that more goods to and from Japan could travel on
Japanese bottoms, It was pointed out that under the present arrange-
ment an appreciable portion of the appropriations for Japan was
merely a hidden subsidy to American shipping.
8.
SCAP TRADE OFFICES
We were in agreament that the SCAP New York Trade Office
should be a trade promotion office and not a sales office. We also
were in agreement that other trade promotion offices should be
opened up in the immediate future, among the first being offices in
Pakistan, India, and Germany.
9. LULTIPLE CURRENCY PROBLEVS IN JAPAN
We were in agreament that there should be a minimum number
of currencies in Japan and that an endeavor should be made to work
toward the wider use of yen in transactions involving licensed con-
cerns with, of course, the proper controls of exchange accounts of
convertible yen strictly maintained. Four currencies are already in
use in Japan and we should try to cut this number down instead of
adding currencies for specific uses.
10. PEARIS
We agreed that the sale of pearls should be still further
unfrozen and that the monopoly or control of the pearl business in
Japan by the Army's Central Purchasing Office should be broken.
David M. Maynard
Economic Counselor

FROM
Tokyo's 457 of July 15, 1949
SUBJECT:
Various economic problems were discussed between Mr. Friele
(CAD, Army) and Mr. Maynard of POLAD, as recorded in a memo of
conversation forwarded hereunder.
Mr. Freile and Mr. Maynard agreed that present Japanese
living standards, compared to pre-war days, are better than 1s
shown by Division reports and statistics.  It was also agreed
that a surplus of production over sales in many lines necessitates
sending out of Japanese salesmen.
Mr. Freile recommended reducing ESS and dividing 1t into
task sections; he also suggested a civilian economic advisor to
coordinate these economic sections. Mr. Maynard further mentioned
placing segments of ESs, NRS, and other Sections under this ad-
visor, thus making local Divisions correspond roughly with those
in Washington.
Mr. Maynard recommended a cut in the number of workers in
econom1c affairs in GHQ by 95% by January 1, 195l, facilitating
use of limited advice with certain financial controls rather than
complete operation of Japanese economy. Mr. Freile generally
agreed.
Both agreed thet bilateral agreements had been necessary in
lieu of other suggestione; trade agreements should make possible
later multilateraliem.
Japanese exporters should be allowed to retain a small per-
centage of foreign exchange, should be allowed to go abroad to
sell, being advanced funds for such travel to be repaid under the
retention system.
A definite Japanese import tariff policy 1s necessary before
MFN bargaining.
More Japanese goods should be carried 1n Japanese bottoms.
New York Office should engage in trade promotion, not sales;
trade promotion offices should also be opened in Pakistan, India
and Germany.
A minimum number of currencies should be used, with use of
yen desirable.
The sale of pearls should be unfrozen and Army's Central Pur-
chasing Office monopoly of pearls broken.

SUBJECT:
Progressive Decontrel of the conomy of Japan.
One of the most iaportant lecicions to be faced is the political
and economic advisability of the United States continuing to heavily
subsidize Japanese econemy.
As lon as United tates appropri ates funis for the use of Japan
it will be necessary to exercise certain controls of the expenditure of
these funds. Mr. DoD.E recently states "that Japan is living beyond its
means". It is firmly believel that this statement is correct and that a
realistic approach to Jap nese economy wouli indicate that Japan, unless
it is to remain a pemanen irain on the kmerican taxpayer, should seek
to esta: lish its economy at a level at which it can be self supporting.
If drastie measures are taken in: cold un! hard facts faced this self-
support should be achievei by he unl f )l at the latest.
Japn has reached the point in he levelooment of rehabilitation
where pesoval  orei n i.e., "oseup ti n" sntrsls of its economy
should pregress as rapilly as poseible. h word "control" here is used
to encompass what is vometimes referred to  proponents of those controls
as "benevolent uidance". It woul: n r ally be to the interest of all
concerned to have an early peace reaty. If, howevor, international com-
plications are such that no treaty can he si ned for several years, it is
strongly urei that orein control  inie to the irreducible min-
imum consistent vith a lisarmed Japan.
In considerin the future economi: controls in Japan one should not
lose si ht of she faet that a an wa net a backward agricultural country
witkout wo lern inhwstpior, to For wxmy le, we see today in the State of
Laos. To the contrary, Japan in l9lo had ohe of the finest merchant
marines in the worli, the best railroa! system in the Far Hast, the finest
steel mills an. cotton mills in Asia. It had highly developed insurance
and banking institutions md tralin, institutions vhich encircled the
lobe.

Japan's ratin as an international credit risk was high, as loans
were serviced and paid. That this reat economic organization played
into the hands of an Iaperialistic Hilitary group, which attempted to
dominate the Far ast and finally launched a war, is a tragic commentary,
but does not modify the extent of the development of prewar Japan or the
ability of the Jasanese to finance their own economy.
On the other hind, one must e fully aware that there is, and always
will be, schools of thought that will endeavor to prove that Japan must
have continuous direction an: financial support rom Anerica for many
decades to cone. In nany cases proponents are uided semi-consciously
by self-intere t, an! in other cases by those en lowed with a missionary
complex a fervent zeal stron in their conviction of the superiority of
merican culture, and their  elief that the wherican way is the answer to
all problems.
A new constituion ha bee rusented to the Japanese with the inter-
vention of JAP. Many laws an: decrees have been passed by the Japanese
either under direct oriers or under the suggestion of GHi. In general,
the tendency has been towand direetin Japan, holding up as a model the
anerican economic, social, iniustrial and legal pattern. In fact, laws
have been passed at the su ge stion of Sections of JQ which have given the
Japanese people rights asi rotection only gained after decades of struggle
in the Inited State. In the lior field, for example, rights were given
to Japanese lator n t only nknon in prewar Japan, but greater than gen-
erally iven t 1 hor in the nited tates. Laws against monopolistic
practices have been ore severe than normally applied in america and the
economic pur e las put a bani f "uilty" without trial on most if not all
of the really reat in mmmtrialists in Japan.
after low yeare  peu aney i i irmly believed that future policy
of the United tateo sholi be to withdraw civilian control from Japanese
"economy as rapidly as possible. The a minist ration ani/or direction by
Jeneral Headquarter: of the operation of the rai lrcads, the building up of
the Merchant Marine, the minin o coal, the weavin of cloth, catching of
fish, the rationin, of ooistu fs, shoul all revert to the Japanese people,
and the secupation civilian advisors shoul: confine themselves to policy
and a few strict financial controls. In other words, the concept of oc-

9. Circular on Forsign Business and Investment Activities:
SCAP Circular no. 2 of January 14, 1949 (our despatch no. 48, January
24, 1949) 1s now in process of revision. Proposed amandments, which
are undergoing lengthy stafr processing, would apply oaly to portion
of Circular covaring business activities. Distinctiom between foreign
nationals resident in Japan on or before September 2, 1945, and those
subsequently admitted is to be abolimhed, while license must be
obtained fron Japanese Government, with concurrence of SCAP, to engage
in banking, inourance, transportation, public utilities, professiomal
sorvicos, the dissenination of education, informational, or cultural
materials from abroad, or othor businesses which may be designated by
SCAP. Proposal has concurrence of thi: Mission, and is considered
stop forward because it abolishes artificial distinction betwoun per-
manent residents and recsnt antrants, and places more responsibility
on Japaneso Government. All businesses other than those listed will
not require license, while would-be businesamen will no longer be
required to provo that their activities will further Japan's econcmic
rehabilitaticn or otharwise advance SCAP objectives. Significant
alco is fact that recipients of foreiga erchange will no longer have
to le liconsed.

 ade arrangament with Sterling Area oontinue to lag. Conferences
bstween SCap and Sterling Area trade eoordinators indicate that un-
